---------- turn 20 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my doctor sent me, mostly. She says my weight’s gotten too low and now everyone’s sort of making a fuss about it. I don’t really see it the same way, I guess… I mean, in the shop, when you’re moving around lumber and tools all day, being lighter feels like it helps me keep up, not get slowed down. I’ve been a carpenter a long time, and you learn pretty quick what makes the day easier, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I think it’s normal enough, yes. I mean, my husband doesn’t agree—he keeps saying I look too thin, and even the apprentices at the shop have started making little comments, which is irritating. They’re young, they don’t know what it’s like to work clean and steady for decades. I tell them I’m fine, honestly. People see discipline and suddenly they want to call it sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say afraid exactly… but yes, I do worry. If I eat something heavy, or those rich holiday foods we used to make—my mother had this stuffing recipe, and pies, all that butter—I can feel myself getting uneasy right away. Like I’m going to gain weight and lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like once I let that go, everything else starts slipping too.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes, but it doesn’t feel like “limiting” so much as keeping the day in order. On workshop days I’ll usually just have tea, maybe a few careful bites of something if I need it. Stopping for a proper meal feels… unnecessary, and it breaks the rhythm. When you’re in the middle of measuring or sanding or trying to get a joint to sit right, you don’t want to get sluggish. And yes, I suppose part of it is not wanting to gain weight, but it’s more that it keeps me in control of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I look too thin. Other people do, apparently—my husband keeps saying it, and the apprentices at the shop have started making remarks, which I don’t appreciate. They’ll look at me like I’m made of kindling or something, and meanwhile half of them can’t measure twice without losing the pencil. I tell them I’m fine. People mistake discipline for being sick, that’s all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check your body shape or weight to make sure you're still thin?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it checking. I mean, I notice things, of course. Anyone notices how their clothes sit or whether a belt feels different. When you work with wood, you’re always noticing small changes—grain, warping, a board that looked straight yesterday and isn’t today—so I suppose that habit carries over a bit.\n\nBut I don’t know what you mean exactly by “make sure.” I’m not standing around admiring myself, if that’s what you mean. I just… notice. Sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control of your life if you were to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it feels that way. Not “my whole life,” maybe, but if I eat something heavy, or those rich things we used to make around the holidays—pastries, casseroles, all that—then right away I start thinking, well, there it goes, I’ve let things slip.\n\nAnd it’s silly, because I used to be the one making half of it, you know, getting the table right, making sure the chairs didn’t wobble, fixing whatever cabinet door someone had broken before guests came over… but now if I have those foods, I feel like I’m losing the grip I worked very hard to keep. Weight feels like part of that grip, I suppose. So yes, I worry I’d lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I see myself as I am. My husband says different, of course—he keeps telling me I look too thin, and the apprentices at the shop have started in with their little comments too. They’ll mutter like I can’t hear them, which is rich, considering last week one of them cut a shelf half an inch short and tried to blame the tape measure.\n\nBut no, I don’t think they’re seeing it right. I think people see someone who’s careful, who has some discipline, and they decide that must mean something is wrong. I feel fine. I don’t look at myself and think, “Oh, that’s too thin.” I think they’re making it into more than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably. It’s not only the weight. On workshop days, especially, I’ll just have tea, maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel I ought to. Stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary, like it breaks the rhythm.\n\nYou know, once I’m measuring and cutting, if I stop too long I lose track of where I was—then someone moves a pencil or puts a clamp back in the wrong place, and the whole day feels sloppy. So I keep it simple. Tea, a bit of something, and I can keep control of the day. A real meal just feels like too much interruption.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your daily life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more the control, if I’m honest. The weight is part of it, yes, but on workshop days I mostly just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something, because stopping for a real meal feels unnecessary.\n\nIt throws everything off. If I sit down properly, then I’m thinking about the time, and whether someone moved the square, or whether the glue-up is going to set right—well, you know, not that you’d be worrying about glue in here, but it’s that sort of thing. The day starts to feel less in my hands. So I keep it small and careful, and then I can keep going.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything besides gaining weight that you're trying to achieve or avoid by limiting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I’m trying to avoid the day getting away from me. On workshop days especially, I’ll just have tea, maybe a few careful bites of something, because stopping for a proper meal feels like this whole production.\n\nIt sounds silly, but if I sit down to eat, then I’m watching the time, and then someone’s used the good chisel for something ridiculous, or the measurements are off, and suddenly everything feels loose. Like I’ve lost the thread. So keeping it small keeps the day… manageable, I suppose. It feels unnecessary to stop for more than that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is a big part of it. If I eat anything heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make—rich things, you know, the kind that sit in you—I start worrying almost right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the weight, exactly, it’s like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. My sister used to make this pudding at Christmas, and everyone would fuss over it, and I’d be thinking about how much was in it instead of just… enjoying the room. So yes, the fear is there. It gets loud very quickly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: No, not the only reason. It’s there, of course, but on workshop days it’s more like… I just don’t want to stop. I’ll usually have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something, because a real meal feels unnecessary, like it interrupts the whole rhythm.\n\nIf I sit down properly, then I’m thinking about the clock, and the clamps, and whether I’ve left something half-fitted—well, that’s not really what you asked, sorry. But yes, it’s also about keeping control of the day. Eating more makes it feel like the day gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to maintain your current weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I mean, there are other bits tangled up in it, but if I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have those holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying almost immediately that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it feels like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. It’s odd, because I can remember the smell of those things in the kitchen more clearly than—well, sorry. But yes, the fear of gaining weight is the main part of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the reason you restrict your food intake, above all other considerations?\n\nPatient: Yes… above other things, I think it is. If I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the holiday foods we used to make—the kind with all the butter and sugar, you know—I start worrying almost right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the number, it’s this feeling that I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. Like once I start, I won’t be able to pull it back. I know I’ve said that before, probably, but that’s what comes up first for me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat as little as you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s the main reason. If I eat anything heavy, or those foods we used to make for holidays—my mother had these tins she only brought down once a year, and I can still picture them—then right away I start worrying I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it feels like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. I know that sounds repetitive, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you eat as little as you do?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, maybe… I mean, there are little rules and habits that get wrapped around it. But the big one, yes, is that fear.\n\nIf I eat something heavy, or let myself have those holiday foods we used to make—I can still see the kitchen table all crowded with tins and flour and things—I start worrying right away that I’m going to gain weight. And then I feel like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s the part that takes over.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it is. I know I keep circling back to it, but if I eat anything heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make—my aunt had this big old tin for cookies, I can still picture it—I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the food, it’s like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s the main thing in my head.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s the only reason exactly… I get caught up in routines too, like how I cut things or avoid certain meals, and then I’m thinking about other things I need to fix around the house—there’s this cabinet door that never hangs straight, and it bothers me more than it should.\n\nBut underneath it, yes, it’s mostly that fear. If I eat anything heavy, or let myself have those holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight… and then I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is the main reason. I mean, there are all these little things that get tangled up with it, and then I start thinking about meals the way I think about measuring a cut wrong at work—once it’s off, it feels like the whole thing’s ruined.\n\nBut really, if I eat anything heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying almost immediately that I’ll gain weight. And then it feels like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.1"], "note": "", "is_final": true}

And then I feel like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s the part that takes over.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it is. I know I keep circling back to it, but if I eat anything heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make—my aunt had this big old tin for cookies, I can still picture it—I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the food, it’s like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s the main thing in my head.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s the only reason exactly… I get caught up in routines too, like how I cut things or avoid certain meals, and then I’m thinking about other things I need to fix around the house—there’s this cabinet door that never hangs straight, and it bothers me more than it should.\n\nBut underneath it, yes, it’s mostly that fear. If I eat anything heavy, or let myself have those holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight… and then I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is the main reason. I mean, there are all these little things that get tangled up with it, and then I start thinking about meals the way I think about measuring a cut wrong at work—once it’s off, it feels like the whole thing’s ruined.\n\nBut really, if I eat anything heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying almost immediately that I’ll gain weight. And then it feels like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep."
